\[ m = \tan(\theta) \]

Where:
- \( \theta \) is the angle made by the line with the positive x-axis.

Given \( \theta = 120^\circ \), let's compute the slope:

1. **Calculate the Tangent of 120 Degrees:**
   
   Since \( 120^\circ \) lies in the second quadrant where tangent values are negative, we can express it as:
   
   \[
   \tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ)
   \]
   
2. **Find \( \tan(60^\circ) \):**

   \[

   \]

   \[

First, let's denote the unknown point on the x-axis as (x, 0). 

Next, I'll calculate the distance from this point to both given points using the distance formula. The distance between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is √[(x2 - x1)^2 + (y2 - y1)^2].

So, the distance from (x, 0) to (9, 8) is:
√[(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2] = √[(9 - x)^2 + 64]

Similarly, the distance from (x, 0) to (5, 6) is:
√[(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2] = √[(5 - x)^2 + 36]

Since these distances are equal, I can set them equal to each other and solve for x:

√[(9 - x)^2 + 64] = √[(5 - x)^2 + 36]

To eliminate the square roots, I'll square both sides of the equation:
(9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36

Expanding both sides:
81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36

Simplifying each side:
145 - 18x + x^2 = 61 - 10x + x^2

Subtracting x^2 from both sides:
145 - 18x = 61 - 10x

Bringing like terms to one side:
-8x = -84

Finally, solving for x gives x = 7.

Therefore, the point on the x-axis that is equidistant from (9, 8) and (5, 6) is (7, 0).

To find a point on the **x-axis** that is equidistant from the points \((9, 8)\) and \((5, 6)\), let's follow these steps:

1. **Let the unknown point be**:  
   Since the point lies on the x-axis, its y-coordinate must be \(0\). Let this point be \((x, 0)\).

2. **Calculate the distances from \((x, 0)\) to both given points**:
   
   - Distance to \((9, 8)\):
     \[
     d_1 = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64}
     \]
     
   - Distance to \((5, 6)\):
     \[
     d_2 = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
     \]

3. **Set the distances equal since the point is equidistant**:
   \[
   \sqrt{(9 - x)^2 + 64} = \sqrt{(5 - x)^2 + 36}
   \]
   
4. **Square both sides to eliminate the square roots**:
   \[
   (9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36
   \]
   
5. **Expand and simplify**:
   \[
   81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36
   \]
   \[
   145 - 18x + x^2 = 61 - 10x + x^2
   \]

6. **Subtract \(x^2\) from both sides**:
   \[
   145 - 18x = 61 - 10x
   \]
   
7. **Solve for \(x\)**:
   \[
   -8x = -84
   \]
   \[
   x = 7
   \]

To find the slope of a line that makes an angle of \(60^\circ\) with the positive y-axis, we start by considering the direction vector of such a line. The angle between this direction vector and the positive y-axis is \(60^\circ\).

We use the dot product formula to determine the relationship between the components of the direction vector \((a, b)\) and the positive y-axis vector \((0, 1)\):

\[
\cos(60^\circ) = \frac{b}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Given that \(\cos(60^\circ) = 0.5\), we have:

\[
0.5 = \frac{b}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

Squaring both sides to eliminate the square root:

\[
0.25 = \frac{b^2}{a^2 + b^2}
\]

Multiplying both sides by \(a^2 + b^2\):

\[
0.25(a^2 + b^2) = b^2
\]

Simplifying this equation:

\[
0.25a^2 + 0.25b^2 = b^2
\]

Subtracting \(0.25b^2\) from both sides:

\[
0.25a^2 = 0.75b^2
\]

Multiplying both sides by \(\frac{4}{3}\):

\[
a^2 = 3b^2
\]

Thus, the ratio of \(a\) to \(b\) is \(\pm\sqrt{3}\). The direction vector can be written as \((\pm\sqrt{3}k, k)\) where \(k\) is a scalar. The slope \(m\) of the line is given by:

\[
m = \frac{b}{a}
\]

Substituting the values of \(a\) and \(b\):

\[
m = \frac{k}{\pm\sqrt{3}k} = \pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = \pm\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}
\]

Therefore, the slope is:

\[
\boxed{\pm \dfrac{\sqrt{3}}{3}}
\]

First, I note that one of the points, (6,0), lies on the x-axis since its y-coordinate is zero.

Next, I calculate the slope between the two points using the formula:

Slope = (y₂ - y₁) / (x₂ - x₁)

Substituting the given coordinates:

Slope = (0 - 1) / (6 - 5) = (-1)/1 = -1

The slope of the line is -1.

To find the angle between the line and the positive direction of the x-axis, I use the arctangent function:

Angle = arctan(Slope)

Therefore,

Angle = arctan(-1) = -45 degrees

Since angles are typically measured in a positive direction from the x-axis, the positive equivalent of this angle is 360 degrees minus 45 degrees, which equals 315 degrees.

Thus, the angle between the line and the x-axis is 315 degrees.

**Final Answer**
The possible values of \( a \) are \(\boxed{\frac{1}{2}}\) and \(\boxed{1}\).
</think>

To solve the problem of finding the value of \( a \), we start by considering two lines with slopes in the ratio 1:2. Let the slopes be \( m \) and \( 2m \). The tangent of the angle \( \theta \) between these two lines is given as \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{3} \).

Using the formula for the tangent of the angle between two lines:
\[
\tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 m_2} \right|
\]
we substitute \( m_1 = m \) and \( m_2 = 2m \):
\[
\frac{1}{3} = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + (m)(2m)} \right|
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
\frac{1}{3} = \left| \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} \right|
\]
Since \( m > 0 \), we can drop the absolute value:
\[
\frac{m}{1 + 2m^2} = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Cross-multiplying gives us the quadratic equation:
\[
3m = 1 + 2m^2
\]
Rearranging terms, we get:
\[
2m^2 - 3m + 1 = 0
\]
Factoring this quadratic equation, we find:
\[
(2m - 1)(m - 1) = 0
\]
This gives us the solutions \( m = \frac{1}{2} \) and \( m = 1 \).

Thus, the possible values of the slopes are 1 and 0.5. Given that the problem mentions "find the value of \( a \)", we consider the intercepts in the equations of the lines. The final answer for the value of \( a \) is either 1 or 0.5.

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{2}} \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{1}
\]

The equations of lines parallel and passing through (0,5) can be represented as \boxed{y = mx + 5}, where \( m \) is the slope of the line.

To find the equations of lines that are parallel and pass through the point (0, 5), we need to consider the concept of parallel lines. Parallel lines have the same slope but different y-intercepts. 

Given a specific slope \( m \), the equation of a line passing through the point (0, 5) can be written using the slope-intercept form:

\[ y = mx + b \]

Since the line passes through (0, 5), we substitute \( x = 0 \) and \( y = 5 \) into the equation to find the y-intercept \( b \):

\[ 5 = m \cdot 0 + b \]
\[ b = 5 \]

Thus, the equation of the line is:

\[ y = mx + 5 \]

Therefore, the general form of the equations of lines parallel and passing through (0, 5) is:

\[
\boxed{y = mx + 5}
\]

To find the equation of a line passing through two points (3, 1) and (5, 7), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Calculate the Slope (m)**  
The formula for slope \( m \) between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

**Step 2: Find the Y-Intercept (b)**  
Using the slope-intercept form of a line \( y = mx + b \), substitute one of the points to solve for \( b \). Let's use the point (3, 1):
\[
1 = 3(3) + b \\
1 = 9 + b \\
b = 1 - 9 \\
b = -8
\]

**Step 3: Write the Equation of the Line**
Substitute \( m = 3 \) and \( b = -8 \) into the slope-intercept form:
\[
y = 3x - 8
\]

This equation represents the line that passes through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7).

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': 'y = 3x - 8'}

Given that a line has an \( x \)-intercept at \( -\frac{5}{4} \) and makes an angle \( \theta \) with the positive direction of the \( x \)-axis such that \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{4} \), we need to find the equation of the line.

1. The slope \( m \) of the line is given by \( \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{4} \).
2. Using the point-slope form of the equation of a line, passing through the \( x \)-intercept \( \left( -\frac{5}{4}, 0 \right) \), we have:
   \[
   y - 0 = \frac{1}{4} \left( x + \frac{5}{4} \right)
   \]
3. Simplifying this, we get:
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{4}x + \frac{5}{16}
   \]
4. Converting this to standard form, multiply through by 16:
   \[
   16y = 4x + 5
   \]
5. Rearranging terms, we get:
   \[
   4x - 16y + 5 = 0
   \]

Thus, the equation of the line is \(\boxed{4x - 16y + 5 = 0}\).

To determine if three points are collinear using their equation of line method, first find the equation of the line passing through any two points.

Let's use points (3, 0) and (-2, -2).

Calculate the slope:

m = (y₂ - y₁)/(x₂ - x₁) = (-2 - 0)/(-2 - 3) = (-2)/(-5) = 2/5

Use point-slope form with (3, 0):

y - 0 = (2/5)(x - 3)

Simplify:

y = (2/5)x - 6/5

Now, check if the third point (8, 2) lies on this line.

Plug x=8 into the equation:

y = (2/5)*8 - 6/5 = 16/5 - 6/5 = 10/5 = 2

Since when x=8, y=2 matches the third point's y-coordinate, all three points lie on the same line.
</think>

To determine if the three points (3, 0), (-2, -2), and (8, 2) are collinear using the equation of a line method, follow these steps:

1. **Find the Equation of the Line Passing Through Two Points:**
   
   Let's choose two points, say (3, 0) and (-2, -2).

2. **Calculate the Slope (\( m \)) Between These Two Points:**
   
   The slope \( m \) is calculated using the formula:
   
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the values:
   
   \[
   m = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Line Using Point-Slope Form:**
   
   The point-slope form of a line is:
   
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using the point (3, 0):
   
   \[
   y - 0 = \frac{2}{5}(x - 3)
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   
   \[
   y = \frac{2}{5}x - \frac{6}{5}
   \]

4. **Verify If the Third Point Lies on This Line:**
   
   Substitute \( x = 8 \) into the equation to find \( y \):
   
   \[
   y = \frac{2}{5}(8) - \frac{6}{5} = \frac{16}{5} - \frac{6}{5} = \frac{10}{5} = 2
   \]
   
   The calculated \( y \)-coordinate is 2, which matches the \( y \)-coordinate of the third point (8, 2).

To solve the problem where both axes are divided by a ratio of 1:4 from the origin, we start by considering a line that passes through points dividing the x-axis and y-axis intercepts in the given ratio.

1. Let the intercepts on the x-axis and y-axis be \(a\) and \(b\) respectively.
2. The points where these axes are divided in the ratio 1:4 from the origin are \((\frac{a}{5}, 0)\) and \((0, \frac{b}{5})\).
3. We need to find the equation of the line passing through these two points.

Using the intercept form of the line equation:
- The x-intercept is \(\frac{a}{5}\), so \(A = \frac{a}{5}\).
- The y-intercept is \(\frac{b}{5}\), so \(B = \frac{b}{5}\).

The equation of the line passing through these intercepts is:
\[
\frac{x}{A} + \frac{y}{B} = 1
\]

Substituting \(A\) and \(B\) into the equation:
\[
\frac{x}{\frac{a}{5}} + \frac{y}{\frac{b}{5}} = 1
\]

Simplifying this, we get:
\[
\frac{5x}{a} + \frac{5y}{b} = 1
\]

Thus, the equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{5x}{a} + \frac{5y}{b} = 1}
\]

Alright, I need to find the equation of a line that is perpendicular to y = 3x + 4 and passes through the point (2, 13). Let me start by recalling some basic concepts.

First, the given line has a slope of 3. Since the new line is perpendicular to this one, its slope should be the negative reciprocal of 3. That means the slope (m) of the new line is -1/3.

Now that I have the slope, I can use the point-slope form of a linear equation to find the equation of the desired line. The formula is y - y1 = m(x - x1), where (x1, y1) is the given point, which is (2, 13).

Substituting the values into the formula: y - 13 = (-1/3)(x - 2). To make it look more standard, I'll multiply both sides by 3 to eliminate the fraction. This gives me 3(y - 13) = -(x - 2). Expanding this, I get 3y - 39 = -x + 2.

Next, I'll rearrange all terms to one side to get the equation into standard form (Ax + By + C = 0). Bringing everything to the left side: x + 3y - 41 = 0. Alternatively, it's often written as x + 3y = 41.

So, the final equation of the line is x + 3y = 41.
</think>

{'Final Answer': 'x + 3y = 41'}

First, I need to determine the slope of the line that makes an angle of \( \frac{2\pi}{3} \) radians (which is 120 degrees) with the positive x-axis. The tangent of this angle will give me the slope.

Calculating \( \tan(120^\circ) \):
\[
\tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ) = -\sqrt{3}
\]

So, the slope \( m \) of the line is \( -\sqrt{3} \).

Next, using the point-slope form of a line equation with the given point (0, 2):
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Substituting \( m = -\sqrt{3} \), \( x_1 = 0 \), and \( y_1 = 2 \):
\[
y - 2 = -\sqrt{3}(x - 0)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
y = -\sqrt{3}\,x + 2
\]

To find where the line intersects the y-axis, I set \( x = 0 \) in the equation. However, since the point (0, 2) is already on the y-axis and lies on the line, there is no need to calculate further.
</think>

Next, plug these values into the distance formula:

Substituting the known values:

Calculating the numerator:

|25 + 42 - 28| = |39| = 39

Calculating the denominator:

Using the formula for the distance between two parallel lines, d = |C₁ - C₂| / √(A² + B²), where A and B are the coefficients of x and y respectively, and C₁ and C₂ are the constants:

1. **Identify the coefficients:**
   - For both equations, A = 3, B = -4.
   - C₁ = 12 (from the first equation) and C₂ = 7 (from the second equation).

   d = |12 - 7| / √(3² + (-4)²)
   
3. **Calculate numerator and denominator:**

   - Denominator = √(9 + 16) = √25 = 5

To determine the equation of the line (side AB) and calculate its length for triangle ABC with vertices at A(4,5), B(6,1), and C(3,4):

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Equation of Line AB:**
   - **Slope Calculation:**  
     The slope \( m \) between points A(4,5) and B(6,1) is calculated as:
     \[
     m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{1 - 5}{6 - 4} = \frac{-4}{2} = -2
     \]
   - **Using Point-Slope Form:**  
     Using point A(4,5) in the equation \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \):
     \[
     y - 5 = -2(x - 4)
     \]
     Simplifying:
     \[
     y - 5 = -2x + 8 \\
     y = -2x + 13
     \]
   
   Therefore, the equation of line AB is \( y = -2x + 13 \).

2. **Length of Side AB:**
   - **Distance Formula:**  
     The distance between A(4,5) and B(6,1) is:
     \[
     d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} \\
     d = \sqrt{(6 - 4)^2 + (1 - 5)^2} \\
     d = \sqrt{2^2 + (-4)^2} \\
     d = \sqrt{4 + 16} \\
     d = \sqrt{20} \\
     d = 2\sqrt{5}
     \]
   
   Therefore, the length of side AB is \( 2\sqrt{5} \).

### Final Answer:

To find the perpendicular bisector (which can also serve as an angle bisector in some contexts) of the line segment between the points \( (5,6) \) and \( (1,4) \), follow these steps:

1. **Find the midpoint**:
   The midpoint formula is \( \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right) \).
   So,
   \[
   M = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
   \]

2. **Calculate the slope of the original line segment**:
   The slope \( m \) between two points is given by \( \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \).
   Here,
   \[
   m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

3. **Determine the slope of the perpendicular bisector**:
   The slope of a line perpendicular to another is the negative reciprocal. So,
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{m} = -2
   \]

4. **Write the equation using point-slope form**:
   Using the midpoint \( (3, 5) \) and the slope \( -2 \),
   \[
   y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
   \]
   
5. **Convert to standard form if needed**:
   Simplifying,
   \[
   y - 5 = -2x + 6 \\
   2x + y = 11
   \]

So, the equation of the perpendicular bisector is \( 2x + y = 11 \).

Answer: The angle bisector (perpendicular bisector) between the points \( (5,6) \) and \( (1,4) \) is given by \( \boxed{2x + y = 11} \).

To find the value of \( \text{હ} \) (denoted as \( H \)) such that the line passing through points \( (H, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \) is perpendicular to the given line \( 7x - 9y - 19 = 0 \), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the slope of the given line:**
   The equation can be rewritten as \( y = \frac{7}{9}x - \frac{19}{9} \). Thus, the slope (\( m_1 \)) is \( \frac{7}{9} \).

2. **Find the slope of the perpendicular line:**
   Since the lines are perpendicular, their slopes multiply to \( -1 \). Therefore, the slope of the new line (\( m_2 \)) is \( -\frac{9}{7} \).

3. **Calculate the slope between the two given points:**
   The slope between \( (H, 5) \) and \( (6, 3) \) is:
   \[
   \text{slope} = \frac{3 - 5}{6 - H} = \frac{-2}{6 - H}
   \]

4. **Set the slopes equal to each other:**
   Set the calculated slope equal to \( -\frac{9}{7} \):
   \[
   \frac{-2}{6 - H} = -\frac{9}{7}
   \]

5. **Solve for \( H \):**
   Cross-multiplying:
   \[
   (-2) \times 7 = (-9) \times (6 - H)
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   -14 = -54 + 9H
   \]
   Add 54 to both sides:
   \[
   40 = 9H
   \]
   Divide by 9:
   \[
   H = \frac{40}{9}
   \]

**Answer:** \( H = \boxed{\dfrac{40}{9}} \)

To find the equation of a line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) that passes through the point \((-4, 5)\), I start by determining the slope of the given line.

First, I rewrite the given equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):

\(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\)

Subtract \(5x\) and 4 from both sides:

\(-6y = -5x - 4\)

Divide each term by \(-6\):

\(y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}\)

From this, I can see that the slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

Since parallel lines have the same slope, the new line will also have a slope of \(\frac{5}{6}\).

Next, I use the point-slope form of a line to find the equation of the new line. The point-slope formula is:

\(y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)\)

Plugging in the values \(m = \frac{5}{6}\), \(x_1 = -4\), and \(y_1 = 5\):

\(y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)\)

I then distribute \(\frac{5}{6}\) on the right side:

\(y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{20}{6}\)

Simplify \(\frac{20}{6}\) to \(\frac{10}{3}\):

\(y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{10}{3}\)

Finally, I add 5 to both sides to get the equation in slope-intercept form:

\(y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}\)
</think>

To find the equation of a line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) that passes through the point \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Find the slope of the given line:**
   
   Start by rewriting the given equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)):
   \[
   5x - 6y + 4 = 0
   \]
   Subtract \(5x\) and 4 from both sides:
   \[
   -6y = -5x - 4
   \]
   Divide each term by \(-6\):
   \[
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]
   The slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

2. **Determine the equation of the parallel line:**
   
   Since parallel lines have the same slope, the new line will also have a slope of \(\frac{5}{6}\).
   
   Use the point-slope form of a line:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   Plug in \(m = \frac{5}{6}\), \(x_1 = -4\), and \(y_1 = 5\):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]
   
3. **Simplify the equation:**
   
   Distribute \(\frac{5}{6}\) on the right side:
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{20}{6}
   \]
   Simplify \(\frac{20}{6}\) to \(\frac{10}{3}\):
   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{10}{3}
   \]
   
4. **Solve for \(y\):**
   
   Add 5 to both sides:
   \[
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The equation of the line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) that passes through \((-4, 5)\) is:

\boxed{\left\{ 'Final Answer' : '\text{\(y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{25}{3}\)}' \right\}}

First, I need to determine if the given lines are parallel. To do this, I'll compare their slopes.

For the first line, 19x + 12y - 38 = 0, I can rewrite it in slope-intercept form:
12y = -19x + 38
y = (-19/12)x + (38/12)

So, the slope (m) is -19/12.

For the second line, 38x + 24y + 70 = 0, I'll do the same:
24y = -38x - 70
y = (-38/24)x - (70/24)

Simplifying the slope gives m = -19/12.

Since both lines have the same slope, they are parallel. This means there's a consistent distance between them across all points on either line.

Next, I'll use the formula to find the distance between two parallel lines:
Distance (d) = |C₁ - C₂| / √(A² + B²)

First, I need to express both equations in the standard form Ax + By + C = 0.

The first equation is already in standard form: 19x + 12y - 38 = 0
Here, A = 19, B = 12, and C₁ = -38

For the second equation:
38x + 24y + 70 = 0
I notice that this can be simplified by dividing all terms by 2:
19x + 12y + 35 = 0
Now, A = 19, B = 12, and C₂ = 35

Plugging these values into the distance formula:
d = |(-38) - 35| / √(19² + 12²)
d = |-73| / √(361 + 144)
d = 73 / √505

I can rationalize the denominator or leave it as is, but for a cleaner look, I'll multiply numerator and denominator by √505:
d = (73√505) / 505
This simplifies to approximately 1.64.

So, the distance between the two parallel lines is approximately 1.64 units.

To find the distance from the origin to each of the given lines and compare them:

### Given Lines:
1. **3x + 4y = 9**
2. **6x + 8y = 18**

### Formula for Distance from a Point to a Line:
The distance \( d \) from a point \( (x_0, y_0) \) to the line \( Ax + By + C = 0 \) is given by:
\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

### Step-by-Step Calculations:

#### For the First Line: \( 3x + 4y = 9 \)
1. **Convert to Standard Form:**  
   Subtract 9 from both sides:
   \[
   3x + 4y - 9 = 0
   \]
2. **Identify Coefficients:**  
   Here, \( A = 3 \), \( B = 4 \), and \( C = -9 \).
3. **Apply the Distance Formula:**  
   The origin is \( (x_0, y_0) = (0, 0) \):
   \[
   d_1 = \frac{|3(0) + 4(0) - 9|}{\sqrt{3^2 + 4^2}} = \frac{|-9|}{5} = \frac{9}{5}
   \]

#### For the Second Line: \( 6x + 8y = 18 \)
1. **Convert to Standard Form:**  
   Subtract 18 from both sides:
   \[
   6x + 8y - 18 = 0
   \]
2. **Identify Coefficients:**  
   Here, \( A = 6 \), \( B = 8 \), and \( C = -18 \).
3. **Apply the Distance Formula:**  
   The origin is \( (x_0, y_0) = (0, 0) \):
   \[
   d_2 = \frac{|6(0) + 8(0) - 18|}{\sqrt{6^2 + 8^2}} = \frac{|-18|}{10} = \frac{9}{5}
   \]

### Conclusion:
Both lines are equidistant from the origin.

To find the equation of the line that passes through the point \( P(4, 1) \) and makes an angle of \( 135^\circ \) with the positive x-axis, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Determine the Slope
The slope \( m \) of a line is equal to the tangent of the angle \( \theta \) it makes with the positive direction of the x-axis:
\[ m = \tan(\theta) \]

Given \( \theta = 135^\circ \):
\[
\tan(135^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 45^\circ) = -\tan(45^\circ) = -1
\]
So, the slope \( m \) is:
\[
m = -1
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation of the Line
The point-slope form of a line equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Where \( (x_1, y_1) \) is a point on the line. Substituting \( P(4, 1) \) and \( m = -1 \):
\[
y - 1 = -1(x - 4)
\]

### Step 3: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation:
\[
y - 1 = -x + 4 \\
y = -x + 5
\]

So, the equation of the line is \( y = -x + 5 \).

I know that \( 120^\circ \) is located in the second quadrant of the unit circle, where tangent values are negative because tangent corresponds to sine over cosine, and in the second quadrant, sine is positive while cosine is negative. So, I can express \( 120^\circ \) as:

\[ 120^\circ = 180^\circ - 60^\circ \]

Using the tangent subtraction formula or recalling that:

\[ \tan(180^\circ - \theta) = -\tan(\theta) \]

\[ \tan(120^\circ) = \tan(180^\circ - 60^\circ) = -\tan(60^\circ) \]

I know that \( \tan(60^\circ) = \sqrt{3} \), which is approximately 1.732.

Therefore,

\[ \tan(120^\circ) = -\sqrt{3} \approx -1.732 \]

This means the slope of the line is negative \( \sqrt{3} \).

***********************<think>
First, I need to understand the problem given.

We have two lines, and we know that the angle between them is π/4 (45 degrees). The slope of one line is 1/4. We are required to find the slope of the other line.

I'll use the formula for the angle θ between two lines with slopes m₁ and m₂:

tan(θ) = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Given that tan(π/4) is 1, I can set up the equation as follows:

|(m₂ - (1/4))/(1 + (1/4)*m₂)| = 1

This leads to two possible equations because of the absolute value:

(m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + m₂/4) = 1 and (m₂ - 1/4)/(1 + m₂/4) = -1

Solving each equation will give me the possible values for m₂.

For the first case:
(m₂ - 1/4) = (1 + m₂/4)
Multiplying both sides by 4 to eliminate fractions:
4m₂ - 1 = 4 + m₂
Subtracting m₂ from both sides and adding 1:
3m₂ = 5 => m₂ = 5/3

For the second case:
(m₂ - 1/4) = -(1 + m₂/4)
Expanding the right side:
m₂ - 1/4 = -1 - m₂/4
Bringing like terms together and multiplying by 4 to eliminate fractions:
4m₂ - 1 = -4 - m₂
Adding m₂ and subtracting -1 from both sides:
5m₂ = -3 => m₂ = -3/5

Therefore, the possible slopes of the other line are 5/3 or -3/5.

Next, I'll find the slope of the second line that passes through the points (10, 14) and (x, 26). Again, using the same slope formula.

After obtaining both slopes, I'll multiply them together. If their product is -1, then the lines are perpendicular, confirming that they intersect at a right angle.

To determine the value of \( x \) such that the two lines intersect at a right angle (90 degrees or "કાટખૂણે" in Hindi), we need to ensure that the slopes of the two lines are negative reciprocals of each other. This means their product should be \(-1\).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Calculate the Slope of the First Line:**

   The first line passes through the points \((0, 8)\) and \((6, 10)\). 

   \[
   \text{Slope}_1 = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{10 - 8}{6 - 0} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
   \]

2. **Determine the Slope of the Second Line:**

   The second line passes through the points \((10, 14)\) and \((x, 26)\). 

   Let’s denote this slope as \( m_2 \).

   \[
   m_2 = \frac{26 - 14}{x - 10} = \frac{12}{x - 10}
   \]

3. **Set Up the Perpendicular Condition:**

   For two lines to be perpendicular, the product of their slopes should be \(-1\).

   \[
   \text{Slope}_1 \times m_2 = -1
   \]
   
   Substituting the values:

   \[
   \frac{1}{3} \times \frac{12}{x - 10} = -1
   \]

4. **Solve for \( x \):**

   Multiply both sides by \( 3(x - 10) \):

   \[
   12 = -3(x - 10)
   \]
   
   Simplify:

   \[
   12 = -3x + 30
   \]

   Subtract 30 from both sides:

   \[
   12 - 30 = -3x
   \]
   
   \[
   -18 = -3x
   \]

   Divide both sides by \(-3\):

   \[
   x = \frac{-18}{-3} = 6
   \]

### Final Answer:

The value of \( x \) is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "6"}
```

First, I need to identify the four vertices of the quadrilateral given their coordinates: (-6, 7), (2, 9), (7, -3), and (-2, 0).

To calculate the area of this polygon, I can use the shoelace formula, which requires listing the coordinates in order either clockwise or counterclockwise and repeating the first vertex at the end to complete the calculation.

Applying the shoelace formula involves multiplying corresponding x and y values and summing them up for two separate calculations. Then, taking the absolute difference between these sums and dividing by 2 will give the area of the quadrilateral.
</think>

To find the **area** of the quadrilateral with vertices at \((-6, 7)\), \((2, 9)\), \((7, -3)\), and \((-2, 0)\) in the Cartesian plane, we can use the **shoelace formula**. This method is effective for calculating the area of a polygon when the coordinates of its vertices are known.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **List the Coordinates:**
   ```
   (x₁, y₁) = (-6, 7)
   (x₂, y₂) = (2, 9)
   (x₃, y₃) = (7, -3)
   (x₄, y₄) = (-2, 0)
   ```
   
2. **Apply the Shoelace Formula:**
   
   The formula is:
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} |(x₁y₂ + x₂y₃ + x₃y₄ + x₄y₁) - (y₁x₂ + y₂x₃ + y₃x₄ + y₄x₁)|
   \]
   
3. **Calculate the Terms:**
   
   - **First Part:**
     \[
     x₁y₂ = (-6)(9) = -54
     \]
     \[
     x₂y₃ = (2)(-3) = -6
     \]
     \[
     x₃y₄ = (7)(0) = 0
     \]
     \[
     x₄y₁ = (-2)(7) = -14
     \]
     
     Sum of the first part:
     \[
     -54 + (-6) + 0 + (-14) = -74
     \]
   
   - **Second Part:**
     \[
     y₁x₂ = (7)(2) = 14
     \]
     \[
     y₂x₃ = (9)(7) = 63
     \]
     \[
     y₃x₄ = (-3)(-2) = 6
     \]
     \[
     y₄x₁ = (0)(-6) = 0
     \]
     
     Sum of the second part:
     \[
     14 + 63 + 6 + 0 = 83
     \]
   
4. **Compute the Area:**
   
   \[
   \text{Area} = \frac{1}{2} |(-74) - (83)| = \frac{1}{2} |-157| = \frac{1}{2} \times 157 = 78.5
   \]
   
### Final Answer:

\boxed{\text{Area of the quadrilateral is }78.5 \text{ square units}}

To find the equation of a line, I can use the point-slope form.

The given slope is -2, and the line passes through the point (0, 5).

Using the formula y - y1 = m(x - x1), where m is the slope and (x1, y1) is the point,

I substitute m with -2, x1 with 0, and y1 with 5.

This gives me y - 5 = -2(x - 0).

Simplifying this equation results in y = -2x + 5.
</think>

To find the equation of a line using the **slope-intercept form**, we can follow these steps:

1. **Identify the slope (m)**:  
   The given slope is **-2**.

2. **Use the point-slope formula**:  
   The general form is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   Where \((x_1, y_1)\) is a point on the line.

3. **Substitute the known values**:  
   Given the point (0, 5):
   \[
   y - 5 = -2(x - 0)
   \]

4. **Simplify the equation**:  
   \[
   y - 5 = -2x
   \]
   Adding 5 to both sides:
   \[
   y = -2x + 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{y=-2x+5}

First, I need to determine the equation of the line passing through the points (3, 1) and (5, 7). 

I'll start by calculating the slope (m) using the formula:
\[ m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \]
Substituting the given points:
\[ m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3 \]

With the slope known, I'll use the point-slope form of a linear equation:
\[ y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \]
Choosing one of the points, for example (3, 1):
\[ y - 1 = 3(x - 3) \]

Finally, I'll simplify this to get the equation in slope-intercept form:
\[ y - 1 = 3x - 9 \]
\[ y = 3x - 8 \]
</think>

To determine the equation of the line passing through the points \( (3, 1) \) and \( (5, 7) \), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope
The slope \( m \) between two points \( (x_1, y_1) \) and \( (x_2, y_2) \) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form
The point-slope form of a linear equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]
Using one of the points, for example \( (3, 1) \):
\[
y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
\]

### Step 3: Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form
Simplify the equation to get it in the form \( y = mx + b \):
\[
y - 1 = 3x - 9 \\
y = 3x - 8
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 8}
\]

   - The y-intercept (\(b\)) is given as \(y = -\frac{5}{4}\).

To determine the equation of the line with a slope \( m = \tan(\theta) = \frac{1}{6} \) and an x-intercept at \( (6, 0) \), we can use the point-slope form of a linear equation:

\[
y = m(x - h)
\]

where:
- \( m = \frac{1}{6} \) is the slope,
- \( (h, k) = (6, 0) \) is the x-intercept.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the given values:**
   - Slope (\( m \)) = \( \frac{1}{6} \)
   - X-intercept = \( (6, 0) \)

2. **Use the point-slope formula:**
   
   Since we know a point through which the line passes (the x-intercept), we can substitute these values into the equation.

   \[
   y = m(x - h) \\
   y = \frac{1}{6}(x - 6)
   \]

3. **Simplify the equation if necessary:**
   
   If required, you can expand and simplify the equation further:

   \[
   y = \frac{1}{6}x - \frac{6}{6} \\
   y = \frac{1}{6}x - 1
   \]

So, the equation of the line is \( y = \frac{1}{6}x - 1 \).

\[
\boxed{\text{Answer: } y = \dfrac{1}{6}x - 1}

To determine if the three points are collinear using the area method, I'll calculate twice the area of the triangle formed by these points.

First, I need to find the coordinates of the points:
Point A is (3, 0), Point B is (-2, -2), and Point C is (8, 2).

The formula for twice the area using the determinant method is:
2 * Area = |(x_A(y_B - y_C) + x_B(y_C - y_A) + x_C(y_A - y_B))|

Plugging in the values:

= |3*(-2 - 2) + (-2)*(2 - 0) + 8*(0 - (-2))|
= |3*(-4) + (-2)*2 + 8*2|
= |-12 - 4 + 16|
= |-16 + 16|
= |0|

Since the result is zero, twice the area is zero. This implies that the three points lie on a straight line and are therefore collinear.
</think>

To determine if the three points (3, 0), (-2, -2), and (8, 2) are collinear using the **area method**, we can use the determinant formula for twice the area of the triangle formed by these points. If the result is zero, the points are collinear.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the coordinates:**
   - Point A: (3, 0)
   - Point B: (-2, -2)
   - Point C: (8, 2)

2. **Use the formula for twice the area of a triangle formed by three points:**
   
   \[
   2 \times \text{Area} = |x_A(y_B - y_C) + x_B(y_C - y_A) + x_C(y_A - y_B)|
   \]
   
3. **Plug in the values:**
   
   \[
   2 \times \text{Area} = |3(-2 - 2) + (-2)(2 - 0) + 8(0 - (-2))|
   \]
   
4. **Calculate each term:**
   
   - First term: \( 3 \times (-4) = -12 \)
   - Second term: \( -2 \times 2 = -4 \)
   - Third term: \( 8 \times 2 = 16 \)

5. **Sum the terms and take absolute value:**
   
   \[
   | -12 - 4 + 16 | = |0| = 0
   \]

### Conclusion:

Since twice the area is zero, the three points lie on a straight line. Therefore, the points are collinear.

\[
\boxed{\text{The points are collinear.}}
\]

Now, let's proceed with the step-by-step explanation and calculations based on the given data.

**Step 1: Identify the Points**

We have two points provided:

- At Price (P₁) = ₹114, Quantity Demanded (Q₁) = 1080 liters
- At Price (P₂) = ₹116, Quantity Demanded (Q₂) = 1320 liters

**Step 2: Calculate the Slope of the Demand Curve**

The slope (b) of the demand curve can be calculated using the formula:

\[ b = \frac{Q₂ - Q₁}{P₂ - P₁} \]

Plugging in the values:

\[ b = \frac{1320 - 1080}{116 - 114} = \frac{240}{2} = 120 \]

So, the slope (b) is 120.

**Step 3: Determine the Intercept (a)**

Using one of the points to find 'a', let's take P₁ and Q₁:

\[ Q₁ = a - bP₁ \]
\[ 1080 = a - 120(114) \]
\[ 1080 = a - 13680 \]
\[ a = 1080 + 13680 = 14760 \]

**Step 4: Formulate the Demand Equation**

Now, we can write the demand equation as:

\[ Qd = 14760 - 120P \]

**Step 5: Calculate Quantity Demanded at P = ₹117**

Substitute P = 117 into the equation:

\[ Qd = 14760 - 120(117) \]
\[ Qd = 14760 - (120 × 117) \]

Calculating \( 120 × 117 \):

- Break down 117 into 100 + 17
- So, \( 120 × 100 = 12000 \)
- \( 120 × 17 = 2040 \) (since 120×10=1200 and 120×7=840; total is 1200+840=2040)
- Adding them together: \( 12000 + 2040 = 14040 \)

Now, subtract:

\[ Qd = 14760 - 14040 = 720 \]

**Conclusion**

At a price of ₹117 per liter, the quantity demanded is **720 liters**.

To find the equation of the line passing through the point (4, 4) with intercepts summing to 13, we start by considering the intercept form of a line:

\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]

where \(a\) is the x-intercept and \(b\) is the y-intercept. Given that the sum of the intercepts is 13, we have:

\[
a + b = 13
\]

Since the line passes through (4, 4), substituting these coordinates into the equation gives:

\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1
\]

We can rewrite this as:

\[
\frac{4(b + a)}{ab} = 1
\]

Substituting \(a + b = 13\) into the equation, we get:

\[
\frac{4 \cdot 13}{ab} = 1 \implies \frac{52}{ab} = 1 \implies ab = 52
\]

We now have two equations:
1. \(a + b = 13\)
2. \(ab = 52\)

These can be solved as a quadratic equation where \(a\) and \(b\) are the roots:

\[
t^2 - 13t + 52 = 0
\]

The discriminant of this quadratic equation is calculated as follows:

\[
D = (-13)^2 - 4 \cdot 1 \cdot 52 = 169 - 208 = -39
\]

Since the discriminant \(D\) is negative, the quadratic equation has no real solutions. Therefore, there are no real intercepts \(a\) and \(b\) that satisfy both conditions.

Thus, the conclusion is that such a line does not exist in the real numbers.

\[
\boxed{\text{No solution exists}}
\]

The equation of the line that passes through the points (1, 0) and (2, 3) can be determined by calculating its slope first. The slope (m) is calculated as:

\( m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = 3 \)

Using the point-slope form with one of the points, for example (1, 0):

\( y - 0 = 3(x - 1) \)
Simplifying this gives:
\( y = 3x - 3 \)

Thus, the equation of the line is:

Given the points (4,7) and (-5,8), we need to find the equation of the line that passes through these points and is perpendicular to another line. 

First, we calculate the slope of the original line connecting the points (4,7) and (-5,8):
\[
\text{slope} = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9}
\]

A line perpendicular to this will have a slope that is the negative reciprocal of -1/9, which is 9.

Next, we find the midpoint of the segment connecting (4,7) and (-5,8):
\[
\text{midpoint} = \left( \frac{-5 + 4}{2}, \frac{8 + 7}{2} \right) = \left( -0.5, 7.5 \right)
\]

Using the midpoint and the slope of 9, we write the equation of the perpendicular bisector using point-slope form:
\[
y - 7.5 = 9(x + 0.5)
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
y = 9x + 4.5 + 7.5
\]
\[
y = 9x + 12
\]

Thus, the equation of the line is \boxed{y = 9x + 12}.

Given the provided coordinates of points \( P(2, 1) \), \( Q(-2, 3) \), and \( R(4, 5) \), we can determine whether a single straight line passes through all three. 

First, calculate the slope between \( P \) and \( Q \):
\[
m_{PQ} = \frac{y_Q - y_P}{x_Q - x_P} = \frac{3 - 1}{-2 - 2} = \frac{2}{-4} = -0.5
\]
Using point-slope form with \( P(2, 1) \):
\[
y - 1 = -0.5(x - 2)
\]
Simplify to get:
\[
y = -0.5x + 2
\]

Next, calculate the slope between \( Q \) and \( R \):
\[
m_{QR} = \frac{y_R - y_Q}{x_R - x_Q} = \frac{5 - 3}{4 - (-2)} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}
\]
Using point-slope form with \( Q(-2, 3) \):
\[
y - 3 = \frac{1}{3}(x + 2)
\]
Simplify to get:
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x + \frac{11}{3}
\]

Since the slopes between \( P \) and \( Q \), as well as \( Q \) and \( R \), are different, all three points do not lie on a single straight line.

\[
y = -0.5x + 2
\]

- For \( Q(-2, 3) \) and \( R(4, 5) \): The equation of the line in standard form is:
\[

\]

y = \frac{1}{3}x + \frac{11}{3}

First, I'll calculate the slope for both possible intercepts.

If the x-intercept is at (5, 0):
Slope (m) = (0 - (-4)) / (5 - 0) = 4/5

The equation of the line can be written as:
y = mx + b
Substituting m and b:
y = (4/5)x - 4

If the x-intercept is at (-5, 0):
Slope (m) = (0 - (-4)) / (-5 - 0) = 4/-5 = -4/5

The equation of the line would be:
y = mx + b
Substituting m and b:
y = (-4/5)x - 4

Therefore, there are two possible equations for the line depending on whether the x-intercept is at (5, 0) or (-5, 0).

To determine the equation of the line that passes through the y-intercept \((0, -4)\) and intersects the x-axis either 5 units to the right or left of the origin, we consider both possibilities:

1. **When the x-intercept is at \((5, 0)\):**
   - Calculate the slope \(m\):
     \[
     m = \frac{0 - (-4)}{5 - 0} = \frac{4}{5}
     \]
   - Use the slope-intercept form \(y = mx + b\) with \(b = -4\):
     \[
     y = \frac{4}{5}x - 4
     \]

2. **When the x-intercept is at \((-5, 0)\):**
   - Calculate the slope \(m\):
     \[
     m = \frac{0 - (-4)}{-5 - 0} = \frac{4}{-5} = -\frac{4}{5}
     \]
   - Use the slope-intercept form with \(b = -4\):
     \[
     y = -\frac{4}{5}x - 4
     \]

Both equations satisfy the given conditions.

To determine the distance from the point (5, -7) to the line 5x - 6y – 28 = 0, I can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line. The formula is |Ax + By + C| divided by the square root of (A squared plus B squared), where A and B are coefficients from the line equation, and C is the constant term.

First, identify the values of A, B, and C from the given line equation: 5x - 6y – 28 = 0. Here, A is 5, B is -6, and C is -28.

Next, plug these values into the distance formula along with the coordinates (5, -7) of the point:
Distance = |(5*5) + (-6*-7) + (-28)| / sqrt(5² + (-6)²)

Calculate the numerator: 25 + 42 - 28 equals 39.

Then calculate the denominator: the square root of (25 + 36), which is the square root of 61.

Finally, divide 39 by the square root of 61 to get the distance.
</think>

To determine the **distance from the point** \((5, -7)\) **to the line** \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\), we can use the formula for the distance from a point to a line in coordinate geometry.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the coefficients and constants:**
   - The equation of the line is \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\).
   - Here, \(A = 5\), \(B = -6\), and \(C = -28\).

2. **Apply the distance formula:**
   
   The formula to find the distance (\(D\)) from a point \((x_1, y_1)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is:
   \[
   D = \frac{|A x_1 + B y_1 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
   \]

3. **Substitute the known values:**
   
   Plugging in \(x_1 = 5\), \(y_1 = -7\):
   \[
   D = \frac{|5(5) + (-6)(-7) + (-28)|}{\sqrt{5^2 + (-6)^2}}
   \]

4. **Calculate the numerator:**
   
   \[
   5(5) = 25 \\
   -6(-7) = 42 \\
   25 + 42 - 28 = 39
   \]
   
   So, the numerator is \(|39| = 39\).

5. **Calculate the denominator:**
   
   \[
   5^2 = 25 \\
   (-6)^2 = 36 \\
   25 + 36 = 61 \\
   \sqrt{61} \approx 7.8102
   \]

6. **Compute the distance:**
   
   \[
   D = \frac{39}{\sqrt{61}} \approx \frac{39}{7.8102} \approx 4.993
   \]
   
   Rounding to three decimal places, \(D \approx 4.993\).

### Final Answer:
The distance from the point \((5, -7)\) to the line \(5x - 6y - 28 = 0\) is approximately:

\[
\boxed{4.993}
\]

To find the distance between two parallel lines, I first need to identify their equations. The given lines are \(3x - 4y + 12 = 0\) and \(3x - 4y + 7 = 0\). 

Since both lines have the same coefficients for x and y, they are indeed parallel.

The formula to calculate the distance between two parallel lines \(Ax + By + C_1 = 0\) and \(Ax + By + C_2 = 0\) is:

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}} \]

Plugging in the values from the given equations:
\(A = 3\), \(B = -4\), \(C_1 = 12\), and \(C_2 = 7\).

Calculating the numerator: \(|12 - 7| = |5| = 5\)

Calculating the denominator: \(\sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5\)

Now, dividing the numerator by the denominator gives:

\[ \text{Distance} = \frac{5}{5} = 1 \]
</think>

To find the distance between two **parallel lines**, we can use the following formula:

**Formula:**
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{|C_1 - C_2|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Where:
- \( A, B \) are the coefficients of \( x \) and \( y \) in the line equations.
- \( C_1 \) and \( C_2 \) are the constants from the two different line equations.

**Given Lines:**
1. \( 3x - 4y + 12 = 0 \)
   - Here, \( A = 3 \), \( B = -4 \), \( C_1 = 12 \)

2. \( 3x - 4y + 7 = 0 \)
   - Here, \( A = 3 \), \( B = -4 \), \( C_2 = 7 \)

**Calculations:**
\[
\text{Numerator} = |C_1 - C_2| = |12 - 7| = 5
\]
\[
\text{Denominator} = \sqrt{A^2 + B^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

**Final Distance:**
\[
\text{Distance} = \frac{\text{Numerator}}{\text{Denominator}} = \frac{5}{5} = 1
\]

**Answer:**

```json
{'Final Answer': '1'}
```

The foot of the perpendicular from the point \((1, 5)\) onto the line \(3x - 4y = 16\) can be found by determining where this perpendicular intersects the given line. 

First, we calculate the slope of the given line:
\[
\text{Slope} = \frac{3}{4}
\]
The slope of the perpendicular line is then:
\[
-\frac{4}{3}
\]

Using the point-slope form for the perpendicular line passing through \((1, 5)\):
\[
y - 5 = -\frac{4}{3}(x - 1)
\]
Solving this along with the original line equation \(3x - 4y = 16\) gives the intersection point. Solving these equations step-by-step results in:
\[
x = \frac{124}{25}, \quad y = -\frac{7}{25}
\]

Thus, the foot of the perpendicular is at:

\[
\boxed{\left( \dfrac{124}{25}, -\dfrac{7}{25} \right)}
\]

Okay, I need to find the equation of the perpendicular bisector of the line segment joining the points (5, 6) and (1, 4). Let me break this down step by step.

First, I remember that a perpendicular bisector is a line that cuts another line segment into two equal parts at 90 degrees. So, to find its equation, I need to determine two things: the midpoint of the original segment and the slope of the perpendicular bisector.

Starting with the midpoint. The midpoint formula is ((x1 + x2)/2, (y1 + y2)/2). Plugging in the given points:

Midpoint = ((5 + 1)/2, (6 + 4)/2) = (6/2, 10/2) = (3, 5).

Okay, so the midpoint is at (3, 5). This point lies on the perpendicular bisector.

Next, I need to find the slope of the original segment. The formula for slope (m) between two points (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is:

m = (y2 - y1)/(x2 - x1)

Plugging in the values:

Slope = (4 - 6)/(1 - 5) = (-2)/(-4) = 0.5.

So, the slope of the original segment is 0.5. The perpendicular bisector will have a slope that's the negative reciprocal of this. The negative reciprocal of 0.5 is:

m_perpendicular = -1/(0.5) = -2.

Now I have the slope of the perpendicular bisector (-2) and a point (3, 5) through which it passes. Using the point-slope form of a line equation:

y - y1 = m(x - x1)

Substituting the values:

y - 5 = -2(x - 3)

Simplifying this to slope-intercept form:

y - 5 = -2x + 6  
y = -2x + 6 + 5  
y = -2x + 11

So, the equation of the perpendicular bisector is y = -2x + 11.
</think>

The equation of the perpendicular bisector can be found by determining the midpoint of the segment and then using the negative reciprocal slope of the original line. Here's how it's done:

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Midpoint:**
   The midpoint formula is \(\left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)\).
   
   Plugging in (5, 6) and (1, 4):
   \[
   \text{Midpoint} = \left( \frac{5 + 1}{2}, \frac{6 + 4}{2} \right) = (3, 5)
   \]

2. **Calculate the Slope of the Original Line:**
   The slope \(m\) between two points is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   So,
   \[
   m = \frac{4 - 6}{1 - 5} = \frac{-2}{-4} = 0.5
   \]

3. **Determine the Perpendicular Slope:**
   The slope of a perpendicular line is the negative reciprocal.
   
   Thus,
   \[
   m_{\text{perpendicular}} = -\frac{1}{0.5} = -2
   \]

4. **Write the Equation Using Point-Slope Form:**
   Using point (3, 5) and slope -2:
   \[
   y - 5 = -2(x - 3)
   \]
   
   Simplifying to slope-intercept form:
   \[
   y = -2x + 11
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
{'Final Answer': 'y = -2x + 11'}

The foot of the perpendicular from point \( A(6,3) \) to the line \( 7x - 9y = 19 \) can be found using the formula for projecting a point onto a line. 

Using the formula:
\[
(x', y') = \left( x_0 - \frac{a(ax_0 + by_0 + c)}{a^2 + b^2},\ y_0 - \frac{b(ax_0 + by_0 + c)}{a^2 + b^2} \right)
\]
where \( a = 7 \), \( b = -9 \), \( c = -19 \), and the point is \( (x_0, y_0) = (6,3) \).

Calculating:
\[
ax_0 + by_0 + c = 7(6) + (-9)(3) - 19 = 42 - 27 - 19 = -4
\]
Then,
\[
x' = 6 - \frac{7(-4)}{7^2 + (-9)^2} = 6 + \frac{28}{130} = \frac{404}{65}
\]
\[
y' = 3 - \frac{-9(-4)}{130} = 3 - \frac{36}{130} = \frac{177}{65}
\]

So, the coordinates are \( \left( \frac{404}{65}, \frac{177}{65} \right) \).

Answer: The foot of the perpendicular is at \( \boxed{\left( \dfrac{404}{65};\,\dfrac{177}{65} \right)} \).

To find the distance between the two parallel lines \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\) and \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Confirm Parallelism
First, ensure that the lines are indeed parallel by checking if their coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) are proportional.

- The first line is \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\).
- The second line can be written as \(38x + 24y + 70 = 0\), which simplifies to \(19x + 12y + \frac{70}{2} = 0\) when divided by 2.

Since the coefficients of \(x\) and \(y\) are proportional (\(38 = 2 \times 19\) and \(24 = 2 \times 12\)), the lines are parallel.

### Step 2: Choose a Point on One Line
Choose any point that lies on one of the lines. Let's choose a point from the first line, \(19x + 12y - 38 = 0\).

- To find a suitable point, let’s solve for \(x\) when \(y = 0\):
  \[
  19x + 12(0) - 38 = 0 \\
  19x = 38 \\
  x = 2
  \]
  
- So, the point is \((2, 0)\).

### Step 3: Use the Distance Formula for Parallel Lines
The distance \(d\) between two parallel lines \(ax + by + c_1 = 0\) and \(ax + by + c_2 = 0\) can be found using the formula:
\[
d = \frac{|c_2 - c_1|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2}}
\]

- For the first line, rewrite it in standard form: 
  \[
  19x + 12y - 38 = 0 \\
  19x + 12y + (-38) = 0
  \]
  
- The second line is:
  \[
  38x + 24y + 70 = 0 \\
  (Divide by 2 to match the coefficients of the first line): \\
  19x + 12y + 35 = 0
  \]
  
- Now, \(c_1 = -38\) and \(c_2 = 35\).

### Step 4: Calculate the Distance
Substitute the values into the distance formula:
\[
d = \frac{|35 - (-38)|}{\sqrt{19^2 + 12^2}} \\
d = \frac{|73|}{\sqrt{361 + 144}} \\
d = \frac{73}{\sqrt{505}} \\
d ≈ \frac{73}{22.47} \\
d ≈ 3.25
\]

### Final Answer

\(24x + 288 = 10y - 40\)

Rearranging it into standard form \(Ax + By + C = 0\):
\(24x - 10y + 328 = 0\)

Now, apply the distance formula from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\), which is:
\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Substituting the known values:
\(A = 24\), \(B = -10\), \(C = 328\)
\(x_0 = -2\), \(y_0 = 2\)

Calculating the numerator:
\(|24*(-2) + (-10)*2 + 328| = | -48 - 20 + 328 | = |260| = 260\)

\(\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2} = \sqrt{576 + 100} = \sqrt{676} = 26\)

Finally, compute the distance:
\(d = \frac{260}{26} = 10\)

To solve the problem of finding the distance from the point \((-2, 2)\) to the line \(24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Simplify the Given Equation
Start by expanding and rearranging the given equation into standard form.

\[
24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4)
\]
\[
24x + 288 = 10y - 40
\]
\[
24x - 10y + 328 = 0
\]

### Step 2: Apply the Distance Formula
The distance \(d\) from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\) is given by:

\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Substituting the values:
- \(A = 24\)
- \(B = -10\)
- \(C = 328\)
- \(x_0 = -2\)
- \(y_0 = 2\)

### Step 3: Calculate the Numerator
\[
|24(-2) + (-10)(2) + 328| = |-48 - 20 + 328| = |260|
\]

### Step 4: Calculate the Denominator
\[
\sqrt{24^2 + (-10)^2} = \sqrt{576 + 100} = \sqrt{676} = 26
\]

### Step 5: Compute the Distance
\[
d = \frac{260}{26} = 10
\]

**Solution:**

To find the **x-intercept** and **y-intercept** of the equation \(12x - 9y = 18\), follow these steps:

1. **Finding the x-intercept:**
   
   The x-intercept occurs where \( y = 0 \).

   Substitute \( y = 0 \) into the equation:
   \[
   12x - 9(0) = 18
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   12x = 18
   \]
   Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   x = \frac{18}{12} = \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   
   So, the **x-intercept** is at \( \left( \frac{3}{2},\ 0 \right) \).

2. **Finding the y-intercept:**
   
   The y-intercept occurs where \( x = 0 \).

   Substitute \( x = 0 \) into the equation:
   \[
   12(0) - 9y = 18
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   -9y = 18
   \]
   Solve for \( y \):
   \[
   y = \frac{18}{-9} = -2
   \]
   
   So, the **y-intercept** is at \( (0,\ -2) \).

3. **Expressing in Intercept Form:**

   The intercept form of a line's equation is:
   \[
   \frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
   \]
   where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept.

   Substitute the values of \( a \) and \( b \):
   \[
   \frac{x}{\frac{3}{2}} + \frac{y}{-2} = 1
   \]
   
   Simplify:
   \[
   \frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

```json
{'Final Answer': 'The equation in intercept form is \( \frac{2x}{3} - \frac{y}{2} = 1 \). The x-intercept is \( \left( \frac{3}{2},\ 0 \right) \) and the y-intercept is \( (0,\ -2) \).'}
```

The given problem requires finding the equation of a line that is equidistant from two parallel lines. The lines are:

- Line1: \(9x + 6y - 7 = 0\)
- Line2: \(3x + 2y + 6 = 0\) (after scaling to match coefficients)

To find the midline, we use the formula for a line equidistant from two parallel lines. The midline can be found by averaging their constants if scaled appropriately.

1. Scale Line2 to have the same coefficients as Line1:
   - Multiply Line2 by 3: \(9x + 6y + 18 = 0\)

2. Use the formula for the midline, which is given by:
   \[
   ax + by + \frac{c_1 + c_2}{2} = 0
   \]
   Substituting the constants from Line1 and scaled Line2:
   - \(c_1 = -7\) and \(c_2 = 18\)
   - Midline equation: \(9x + 6y + \frac{(-7) + 18}{2} = 0\)

3. Simplify the midline equation:
   - \[
     9x + 6y + \frac{11}{2} = 0
     \]
   To eliminate the fraction, multiply all terms by 2:
   - \(18x + 12y + 11 = 0\)

Thus, the equation of the midline is:

\[
\boxed{18x + 12y + 11 = 0}
\]

To determine the slope of the line passing through the points (6, -4) and (-2, 8), I can use the slope formula.

First, identify the coordinates:
\( x_1 = 6 \)
\( y_1 = -4 \)
\( x_2 = -2 \)
\( y_2 = 8 \)

Next, apply the slope formula:
\( m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} \)

Substitute the values into the formula:
\( m = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2} \)
</think>

To find the slope (\(m\)) of the line passing through the points \( (6, -4) \) and \( (-2, 8) \), we use the slope formula:

\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the coordinates of the two points:**
   \[
   (x_1, y_1) = (6, -4)
   \]
   \[
   (x_2, y_2) = (-2, 8)
   \]

2. **Plug the values into the slope formula:**
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{8 - (-4)}{-2 - 6}
   \]

3. **Simplify the numerator and denominator:**
   \[
   m = \frac{8 + 4}{-8} = \frac{12}{-8}
   \]

4. **Calculate the slope:**
   \[
   m = -\frac{12}{8} = -\frac{3}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-\dfrac{3}{2}}

To solve this problem, I need to find a point on the x-axis that is equidistant from two given points: (9,8) and (5,6). Let's denote the desired point on the x-axis as (x, 0).

Distance from (x, 0) to (9,8): √[(9 - x)^2 + (8 - 0)^2] = √[(9 - x)^2 + 64]

Distance from (x, 0) to (5,6): √[(5 - x)^2 + (6 - 0)^2] = √[(5 - x)^2 + 36]

Since these distances are equal, I can set up the equation:

√[(9 - x)^2 + 64] = √[(5 - x)^2 + 36]

To eliminate the square roots, I'll square both sides of the equation:

(9 - x)^2 + 64 = (5 - x)^2 + 36

Expanding both sides:

81 - 18x + x^2 + 64 = 25 - 10x + x^2 + 36

Combine like terms on each side:

(81 + 64) - 18x + x^2 = (25 + 36) - 10x + x^2
145 - 18x + x^2 = 61 - 10x + x^2

Subtracting x^2 from both sides:

145 - 18x = 61 - 10x

Now, I'll bring like terms together by adding 18x to both sides and subtracting 61 from both sides:

145 - 61 = 18x - 10x
84 = 8x

Finally, solving for x:

x = 84 / 8 = 10.5

So, the point on the x-axis that is equidistant from (9,8) and (5,6) has an x-coordinate of 10.5.

\]

\]

\]
\[

First, I need to understand the problem. There are two lines where one has twice the slope of the other, and the angle between them is 1/3 radians.

I'll use the formula for the angle between two lines given their slopes:

Plugging these into the formula:
tan(1/3) = |(2m - m)/(1 + m*(2m))|
Simplify:
tan(1/3) = |m| / (1 + 2m²)

Since tan(1/3) is positive, we can write:
m / (1 + 2m²) = tan(1/3)

This gives us an equation to solve for m.

After finding the value of m, I'll calculate the angle each line makes with the x-axis using arctan(m) and arctan(2m).

Finally, the difference between these two angles will give me the required "રેખાઓની op ફોળાવ" which is the angle between the two lines.

To solve the problem, we'll follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   - We have two lines with slopes \( m \) and \( 2m \).
   - The angle between them is given as \( \frac{1}{3} \) radians.
   - We need to find the difference (angle) between their directions, which can be calculated using the arctangent of their slopes.

2. **Apply the Formula for the Angle Between Two Lines:**
   
   The formula to find the angle \( \theta \) between two lines with slopes \( m_1 \) and \( m_2 \) is:
   \[
   \tan(\theta) = \left| \frac{m_2 - m_1}{1 + m_1 \cdot m_2} \right|
   \]
   
   In our case, \( m_1 = m \) and \( m_2 = 2m \), so:
   \[
   \tan\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = \left| \frac{2m - m}{1 + m \cdot 2m} \right|
   \]
   
   Simplifying the equation:
   \[
   \tan\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) = \frac{m}{1 + 2m^2}
   \]

3. **Solve for \( m \):**
   
   Rearranging the equation to solve for \( m \):
   \[
   m = (1 + 2m^2) \cdot \tan\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)
   \]
   
   However, solving this analytically is complex. Instead, we'll use numerical methods or a calculator to find the value of \( m \).

4. **Calculate the Angles:**
   
   Once we have \( m \), we can find the angles each line makes with the x-axis using:
   \[
   \theta_1 = \arctan(m)
   \]
   \[
   \theta_2 = \arctan(2m)
   \]
   
   The difference between these two angles will give us the required angle between the lines.

5. **Final Calculation:**
   
   After finding \( m \) numerically, compute:
   \[
   \text{Angle Difference} = |\theta_2 - \theta_1|
   \]

**Note:** Without a numerical value for \( m \), we can't provide an exact numeric answer here. You would typically use a calculator or software to solve for \( m \) numerically and then compute the angle difference.

For the purpose of this example, assume that after calculating:
\[
m = 0.346
\]

Then:
\[
\theta_1 = \arctan(0.346) \approx 0.327 \text{ radians}
\]
\[
\theta_2 = \arctan(0.692) \approx 0.615 \text{ radians}
\]

So, the angle difference is:
\[
|\theta_2 - \theta_1| \approx 0.615 - 0.327 = 0.288 \text{ radians}
\]

But since we don't have precise numerical values for \( m \), this is an illustrative example.

To determine the equations of lines that are **parallel** and pass through the point \((0, 5)\), we need additional information such as another point or a slope value. However, since you've only provided one point, it's not possible to uniquely determine these lines without further data.

Here’s how you would typically approach this problem:

1. **Identify Additional Information Needed:**
   - If given the slope of the line(s), use the point-slope form.
   - If given another point through which the line passes, calculate the slope first using two points.

2. **Example Using a Slope:**
   
   Suppose you are given the slope \(m = 3\). 

   - **Step 1:** Use the slope-intercept form of a line equation:
     \[
     y = mx + b
     \]
   - **Step 2:** Substitute the known point \((0, 5)\) into the equation to find \(b\):
     \[
     5 = 3(0) + b \\
     \Rightarrow b = 5
     \]
   - **Final Equation:**
     \[
     y = 3x + 5
     \]

   This is one of infinitely many lines that can be drawn through the point \((0, 5)\), each corresponding to a different slope.

**Conclusion:**

Without additional information (such as another point or a specific slope), we cannot provide a unique equation for these parallel lines. Each line requires knowing either its slope or another distinct point it passes through.

To find the equation of the line passing through the points \((3, 1)\) and \((5, 7)\), follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Slope (\(m\))**:
   The formula for the slope between two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is:
   \[
   m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
   \]
   
   Substituting the given points:
   \[
   m = \frac{7 - 1}{5 - 3} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
   \]

2. **Use Point-Slope Form**:
   The point-slope form of a line's equation is:
   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]
   
   Using the point \((3, 1)\) and the calculated slope \(m = 3\):
   \[
   y - 1 = 3(x - 3)
   \]

3. **Simplify to Slope-Intercept Form**:
   To express the equation in the slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)), simplify the equation:
   \[
   \begin{align*}
   y - 1 &= 3x - 9 \\
   y &= 3x - 9 + 1 \\
   y &= 3x - 8
   \end{align*}
   \]

4. **Final Equation**:
   The equation of the line in slope-intercept form is:
   \[
   y = 3x - 8
   \]

\boxed{y = 3x - 8}

To determine if the points \((3, 0)\), \((-2, -2)\), and \((8, 2)\) are **collinear** (lie on the same straight line), we can use the concept of slope.

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope Between Two Pairs of Points

#### Slope Between \((3, 0)\) and \((-2, -2)\):
\[
\text{Slope}_1 = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{-2 - 0}{-2 - 3} = \frac{-2}{-5} = \frac{2}{5}
\]

#### Slope Between \((-2, -2)\) and \((8, 2)\):
\[
\text{Slope}_2 = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1} = \frac{2 - (-2)}{8 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{10} = \frac{2}{5}
\]

### Step 2: Compare the Slopes

Since both slopes (\(\text{Slope}_1\) and \(\text{Slope}_2\)) are equal, this indicates that all three points lie on the same straight line.

### Conclusion
The three points **are collinear** because they have the same slope when connected in pairs. Therefore, the final answer is:

I can calculate the slope (m) of the demand curve using these points:

Slope = (Change in Quantity Demanded) / (Change in Price)
Slope = (1440 - 1320) / (116 - 108) 
Slope = 120 / 8
Slope = 15

Next, I'll find the y-intercept (b) using one of the data points:

1320 = 15 * 108 + b
1320 = 1620 + b
b = -300

Now, the demand equation is:
Quantity Demanded = 15P - 300

Finally, substituting P = ₹117 into the equation:

Q = 15 * 117 - 300 
Q = 1755 - 300 
Q = 1455 liters

To solve a quadratic equation of the form \( ax^2 + bx + c = 0 \), we use the quadratic formula:

\[
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}
\]

Here are the steps to apply this formula:

1. **Identify Coefficients**: Determine the values of \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) from the given equation.
   
2. **Compute the Discriminant**: Calculate the discriminant \( D = b^2 - 4ac \). The nature of the roots depends on the value of \( D \):
   - If \( D > 0 \): Two distinct real roots.
   - If \( D = 0 \): One real root (a double root).
   - If \( D < 0 \): Two complex conjugate roots.

3. **Apply the Quadratic Formula**: Substitute the values of \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) into the formula to find the solutions for \( x \).

**Example**:
Solve the quadratic equation \( x^2 - 5x + 6 = 0 \).

1. Coefficients: \( a = 1 \), \( b = -5 \), \( c = 6 \).
2. Discriminant: \( D = (-5)^2 - 4(1)(6) = 25 - 24 = 1 \). Since \( D > 0 \), there are two real roots.
3. Solutions:
   \[
   x = \frac{5 \pm \sqrt{1}}{2} = \frac{5 + 1}{2} = 3 \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{5 - 1}{2} = 2
   \]
   
**Final Answer**: The solutions are \( x = 3 \) and \( x = 2 \).

Using this method, you can solve any quadratic equation by computing its discriminant and applying the quadratic formula accordingly.

Given the problem of finding a line with x-intercept \(a\) and y-intercept \(b\) such that \(a + b = 13\) and passing through the point \((4, 4)\), we start by writing the equation of the line in intercept form:

\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]

Since the line passes through the point \((4, 4)\), substituting \(x = 4\) and \(y = 4\) gives:

\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{b} = 1
\]

We also know that \(a + b = 13\). Let \(b = 13 - a\). Substituting this into the equation, we get:

\[
\frac{4}{a} + \frac{4}{13 - a} = 1
\]

To solve this, we find a common denominator and combine the fractions:

\[
\frac{4(13 - a) + 4a}{a(13 - a)} = 1
\]

Simplifying the numerator:

\[
\frac{52 - 4a + 4a}{a(13 - a)} = 1 \implies \frac{52}{a(13 - a)} = 1
\]

This leads to the equation:

\[
52 = a(13 - a)
\]

Rewriting it as a quadratic equation:

\[
a^2 - 13a + 52 = 0
\]

Calculating the discriminant:

\[
D = (-13)^2 - 4 \cdot 1 \cdot 52 = 169 - 208 = -39
\]

Since the discriminant is negative, there are no real solutions for \(a\) and \(b\). Therefore, such a line with real intercepts does not exist.

\[
\boxed{\text{No solution}}
\]

To find the equation of the line passing through the points (1, 0) and (2, 3), I'll start by calculating the slope.

First, determine the change in y by subtracting the y-coordinates of the two points. Then, determine the change in x by subtracting the corresponding x-coordinates.

Next, use these changes to calculate the slope using the formula:

slope = (change in y) / (change in x)

Once I have the slope, I'll use the point-slope form of a line equation with one of the given points. This will help me find the y-intercept and write the complete equation of the line.
</think>

To determine the equation of the line passing through the points \((1, 0)\) and \((2, 3)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Calculate the Slope (m)
The slope \(m\) of a line through two points \((x_1, y_1)\) and \((x_2, y_2)\) is given by:
\[
m = \frac{y_2 - y_1}{x_2 - x_1}
\]

Substituting the given points:
\[
m = \frac{3 - 0}{2 - 1} = \frac{3}{1} = 3
\]

### Step 2: Use Point-Slope Form to Find the Equation
The point-slope form of a line equation is:
\[
y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
\]

Using point \((1, 0)\) and the calculated slope \(m = 3\):
\[
y - 0 = 3(x - 1)
\]
Simplifying:
\[
y = 3x - 3
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the line is:
\[
\boxed{y = 3x - 3}
\]

First, I need to find the equation of the line that passes through the points (4,7) and (-5,8).

Substituting the given points:
\( m = \frac{8 - 7}{-5 - 4} = \frac{1}{-9} = -\frac{1}{9} \)

With the slope known, I can use the point-slope form of a line to find the equation. Let's use the point (4,7):

\( y - 7 = -\frac{1}{9}(x - 4) \)

To convert this into the standard form \( Ax + By + C = 0 \), I'll eliminate the fraction by multiplying both sides by 9:

\( 9(y - 7) = - (x - 4) \)
\( 9y - 63 = -x + 4 \)

Bringing all terms to one side:
\( x + 9y - 67 = 0 \)

Thus, the equation of the line in standard form is \( x + 9y - 67 = 0 \).

- Denominator: \( \sqrt{25 + 36} = \sqrt{61} \)

To solve the problem of finding the foot of the perpendicular from the point (1, 5) to the line 3x - 4y - 16 = 0, I will follow these steps:

First, I'll represent the given line in slope-intercept form by solving for y. This will allow me to determine its slope.

Next, since the perpendicular from the point (1, 5) must intersect this line at a right angle, I'll find the equation of the line that is perpendicular and passes through (1, 5). The slope of this perpendicular line will be the negative reciprocal of the original line's slope.

After obtaining both equations of the original line and the perpendicular line, I'll solve these two equations simultaneously to find their intersection point. This intersection point represents the foot of the perpendicular from (1, 5) onto the given line.
</think>

To find the foot of the perpendicular from the point \((1, 5)\) to the line \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Slope of the Given Line
The given line is:
\[ 3x - 4y - 16 = 0 \]

Rearrange it into slope-intercept form \( y = mx + b \):
\[
\begin{align*}
-4y &= -3x + 16 \\
y &= \frac{3}{4}x - 4
\end{align*}
\]
So, the slope (\(m_{\text{line}}\)) is:
\[ m_{\text{line}} = \frac{3}{4} \]

### Step 2: Determine the Slope of the Perpendicular Line
The slope of a line perpendicular to another line with slope \(m\) is given by:
\[ m_{\perp} = -\frac{1}{m} \]

Therefore, the slope of the perpendicular line from \((1, 5)\) is:
\[ m_{\perp} = -\frac{4}{3} \]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Perpendicular Line
Using the point-slope form of a line equation \( y - y_1 = m(x - x_1) \), where the point is \((1, 5)\):
\[
y - 5 = -\frac{4}{3}(x - 1)
\]

Simplify to slope-intercept form:
\[
y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{4}{3} + 5 \\
y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3}
\]

### Step 4: Find the Intersection Point
Now, solve the system of equations to find where these two lines intersect:
\[
\begin{cases}
y = \frac{3}{4}x - 4 \\
y = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3}
\end{cases}
\]

Set the two equations equal to each other:
\[
\frac{3}{4}x - 4 = -\frac{4}{3}x + \frac{19}{3}
\]

Multiply both sides by \(12\) (the least common multiple of denominators \(4\) and \(3\)) to eliminate fractions:
\[
9x - 48 = -16x + 76
\]

Combine like terms:
\[
25x = 124 \\
x = \frac{124}{25} \\
x = 4.96
\]

Substitute \(x\) back into one of the original equations to find \(y\):
\[
y = \frac{3}{4}(4.96) - 4 \\
y = 3.72 - 4 \\
y = -0.28
\]

### Step 5: Final Answer
The foot of the perpendicular from the point \((1, 5)\) to the line \(3x - 4y - 16 = 0\) is:
\[
\left( \frac{124}{25}, -\frac{7}{25} \right)
\]
In decimal form:
\[
\boxed{\left(4.96, -0.28\right)}
\]

= 2/√3

Wait, that simplifies to 1/√3. So tanθ is 1/√3.

The angle between the two lines √3x + y = 1 and x + √3y = 1 is found by first determining their slopes, m₁ = -√3 and m₂ = -1/√3. Using the formula for the tangent of the angle between them:

tanθ = |(m₂ - m₁)/(1 + m₁*m₂)|

Substituting the values:

tanθ = |(-1/√3 - (-√3))/(1 + (√3)(-1/√3))|
= |(2/√3)/2| 
= 1/√3

Since tanθ = 1/√3 corresponds to θ = 30°, the angle between the lines is **30 degrees**.

To find the equation of a line parallel to \(5x - 6y + 4 = 0\) and passing through \((-4, 5)\), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the Slope (m):**

   Start by rewriting the given equation in slope-intercept form (\(y = mx + b\)).

   \[
   5x - 6y + 4 = 0 \\
   -6y = -5x - 4 \\
   y = \frac{5}{6}x + \frac{2}{3}
   \]

   The slope (\(m\)) of the given line is \(\frac{5}{6}\).

2. **Equation of the Parallel Line:**

   Since parallel lines have the same slope, the desired line will also have a slope of \(\frac{5}{6}\). Using the point-slope form:

   \[
   y - y_1 = m(x - x_1)
   \]

   Plug in \(m = \frac{5}{6}\) and the point \((-4, 5)\):

   \[
   y - 5 = \frac{5}{6}(x + 4)
   \]

3. **Convert to Standard Form:**

   Multiply both sides by 6 to eliminate fractions:

   \[
   6(y - 5) = 5(x + 4) \\
   6y - 30 = 5x + 20 \\
   5x - 6y + 50 = 0
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{5x - 6y + 50 = 0}

**Solution:**

We are given the equation:
\[ 24(x + 12) = 10(y - 4) \]

Our goal is to find the distance between the point \((-2, 2)\) and another point whose coordinates we will determine.

### Step 1: Express \(y\) in Terms of \(x\)

First, let's solve for \(y\) in terms of \(x\) using the given equation:

\[
\begin{align*}
24(x + 12) &= 10(y - 4) \\
24x + 288 &= 10y - 40 \quad &\text{(Distribute on both sides)} \\
24x + 328 &= 10y \quad &\text{(Add 40 to both sides)} \\
y &= \frac{24}{10}x + \frac{328}{10} \quad &\text{(Divide both sides by 10)} \\
y &= 2.4x + 32.8
\end{align*}
\]

### Step 2: Find the Coordinates of the Second Point

We are given \(x = -2\). Substitute this into the equation we found:

\[
\begin{align*}
y &= 2.4(-2) + 32.8 \\
y &= -4.8 + 32.8 \\
y &= 28
\end{align*}
\]

So, the coordinates of the second point are \((-2, 28)\).

### Step 3: Calculate the Distance Between the Two Points

Now, we'll use the distance formula to find the distance between \((-2, 2)\) and \((-2, 28)\):

\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]

Substitute the coordinates:

\[
\begin{align*}
\text{Distance} &= \sqrt{(-2 - (-2))^2 + (28 - 2)^2} \\
&= \sqrt{(0)^2 + (26)^2} \\
&= \sqrt{0 + 784} \\
&= \sqrt{784} \\
&= 28
\end{align*}
\]

### Final Answer

The distance between the points is:

Sure, let's solve the problem step-by-step.

**Given Equation:**
\[ 6x + 4y - 24 = 0 \]

**Step 1: Convert to Intercept Form**

The intercept form of a linear equation is:
\[
\frac{x}{a} + \frac{y}{b} = 1
\]
where \( a \) is the x-intercept and \( b \) is the y-intercept.

Starting with the given equation:
\[ 6x + 4y - 24 = 0 \]

Add 24 to both sides:
\[ 6x + 4y = 24 \]

Divide every term by 24 to get it into intercept form:
\[
\frac{6x}{24} + \frac{4y}{24} = \frac{24}{24}
\]
Simplify the fractions:
\[
\frac{x}{4} + \frac{y}{6} = 1
\]

**Step 2: Identify Intercepts**

From the intercept form, we can directly read off the intercepts:
- **X-intercept (\( a \))**: When \( y = 0 \), \( x = 4 \). So, the point is \( (4, 0) \).
- **Y-intercept (\( b \))**: When \( x = 0 \), \( y = 6 \). So, the point is \( (0, 6) \).

**Final Answer:**

\text{X-intercept: } (4, 0) \\
\text{Y-intercept: } (0, 6)
}

The distance from the point (1, 2) to the line \(4x + 7y + 5 = 0\) can be calculated using the formula for the distance from a point \((x_0, y_0)\) to the line \(Ax + By + C = 0\):

\[
d = \frac{|A x_0 + B y_0 + C|}{\sqrt{A^2 + B^2}}
\]

Substituting the given values:
- \(x_0 = 1\)
- \(y_0 = 2\)
- \(A = 4\), \(B = 7\), and \(C = 5\)

Calculate the numerator:
\[
|4(1) + 7(2) + 5| = |4 + 14 + 5| = |23| = 23
\]

Calculate the denominator:
\[
\sqrt{4^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{16 + 49} = \sqrt{65}
\]

So, the distance is:
\[
d = \frac{23}{\sqrt{65}}
\]

To rationalize the denominator:
\[
d = \frac{23 \sqrt{65}}{65}
\]
